aking democracy from just the political

part what was special and important to

them to Future generations and to

posterity my own grandmother

ome thinkers on the right like Hayek

ou know poor and devastated by War and

ourselves from what we morally believe

right

I would say that the Soviet experiment

started off by in Lenin's time as the

attempt to kind of just hold a holding

00:28:08 --> 00:28:12
action

00:28:09 --> 00:28:14
hey we don't really have the conditions

to rule this country we have the support

of the working class or most of it but

he working class is only you know three

percent of the the population you know

00:28:20 --> 00:28:25
the peasantry is really against us a lot

00:28:23 --> 00:28:27
of this three percent of the population

has died in war and half of them

he alternative in their minds was going

00:28:49 --> 00:28:54
all right we're gonna have a holding

00:28:50 --> 00:28:58

possible we'll try to slowly Implement

00:29:00 --> 00:29:05
changes restabilize the economy through

something called a new economic program

which was kind of a form of social

00:29:07 --> 00:29:13
democracy if you if you will because it

allowed market exchange for the peasants

00:29:12 --> 00:29:18
um combined with State ownership of

Industries in the cities and

for a while it seemed to be working the

revolution never came that they were

00:29:21 --> 00:29:26
xpecting in Western Europe but in

00:29:24 --> 00:29:28
Russia itself they were able to

00:29:25 --> 00:29:30
restabilize things by the middle or end

of the the 1920s and they were able to

00:29:31 --> 00:29:38
their policies because people who had

00:29:33 --> 00:29:40

00:29:37 --> 00:29:43
Revolution and then Civil War kind of

00:29:39 --> 00:29:45
just wanted stability and after a decade

00:29:42 --> 00:29:48
plus a war if you had a government that

them

then Stalin came into power and he

logic was the revolution's not going to

00:29:58 --> 00:30:03
come in the west we need to build

ourselves into industrial Powerhouse as

and that's where you got forced

00:30:09 --> 00:30:15
collectivization to try to increase the

00:30:12 --> 00:30:18
productivity of Russian agriculture

00:30:14 --> 00:30:21
through State ownership of previously

00:30:17 --> 00:30:23
fragmented agricultural Holdings and

00:30:20 --> 00:30:25
through the

00:30:22 --> 00:30:27

00:30:24 --> 00:30:28
in more machines to make agriculture

00:30:26 --> 00:30:30

00:30:27 --> 00:30:33
ownership

lus more ambitious attempts to build

00:30:34 --> 00:30:39
now

00:30:35 --> 00:30:41
I say this kind of coolly but we know in

practice what that meant you know forced

00:30:40 --> 00:30:46
collectivization was a disaster I mean

00:30:43 --> 00:30:49
first of all I think was built on the

00:30:45 --> 00:30:51

00:30:48 --> 00:30:53

especially in agriculture but in any

00:31:00 --> 00:31:05
um industrialization uh in the way that

it happened under Stalin also kind of

00:31:04 --> 00:31:11
unbalanced the Soviet economy to lean

um but

00:31:15 --> 00:31:23
his did mean especially the um The

00:31:19 --> 00:31:26
Five-Year Plan in industrialization did

manage to put Russia on a different

so

by the time the post-war period came

the one it might have gave them the

ability to survive the Nazi invasion to

movement like it used to be more rooted

03:30:55 --> 03:31:01
in the working class so if anyone

03:30:57 --> 03:31:03
rewinds the tape may go to when we first

03:31:00 --> 03:31:05
arted talking about early socialism

03:31:02 --> 03:31:09
when I was talking about the German

03:31:04 --> 03:31:11
Social Democratic workers movement or

03:31:08 --> 03:31:13
um all these different early parties I

03:31:10 --> 03:31:16
think at various points I use the word

03:31:12 --> 03:31:19
worker and socialist movement

03:31:15 --> 03:31:21
interchangeably because in fact at the

03:31:18 --> 03:31:24
time it was pretty interchangeable

03:31:20 --> 03:31:26
um socialism was the ideology that had

03:31:23 --> 03:31:27
the appeal of the working class movement

03:31:25 --> 03:31:29
you know

03:31:27 --> 03:31:30
um you couldn't really separate between

03:31:28 --> 03:31:33
the two

03:31:30 --> 03:31:36
um now obviously socialism is like a

03:31:32 --> 03:31:38
fringe ideological current among a very

03:31:35 --> 03:31:40
small minority of the working class

03:31:37 --> 03:31:42
um which is fine but we need to get to

03:31:39 --> 03:31:44
the point I think ideally where when

03:31:41 --> 03:31:46
people talk about unions and people

03:31:43 --> 03:31:49
protesting and social movements and

03:31:45 --> 03:31:51
socialism they all kind of are one and

03:31:48 --> 03:31:53
the same as part of the same broad broad

03:31:50 --> 03:31:55
movement how did you become a socialist

03:31:52 --> 03:31:58
what was what was the personal story

03:31:54 --> 03:31:59
where the idea took hold in your mind so

03:31:57 --> 03:32:01
I'm the youngest of five I was the only

03:31:58 --> 03:32:03
one on my family born in the United

03:32:00 --> 03:32:05
States

03:32:02 --> 03:32:07
um so it was very obvious to me that my

03:32:04 --> 03:32:09
life outcomes were very different than

03:32:06 --> 03:32:12
life outcomes of my siblings so my three

03:32:08 --> 03:32:15
older siblings you know didn't go to

03:32:11 --> 03:32:16
college after After High School some of

03:32:14 --> 03:32:19
them got their degrees

03:32:15 --> 03:32:23
um you know much later on as adults

03:32:18 --> 03:32:24
um but I was from a pretty young age had

03:32:22 --> 03:32:26
access to a great Public School District

03:32:23 --> 03:32:28
and

03:32:25 --> 03:32:29
um was put on the track that you're

03:32:27 --> 03:32:32
going to go to college you know this

03:32:28 --> 03:32:34
is kind of the the outcome and like

03:32:31 --> 03:32:35
I said even my my grandmother was

03:32:33 --> 03:32:37
illiterate

03:32:34 --> 03:32:40
um my mom didn't have a lot of

03:32:36 --> 03:32:42
educational opportunities early

03:32:39 --> 03:32:44
um you know in in her in her life she

03:32:41 --> 03:32:47
actually graduated from college the same

03:32:43 --> 03:32:50
year I did so she she later got her kind

03:32:46 --> 03:32:51
of degrees and and whatever else but

03:32:49 --> 03:32:53
um

03:32:50 --> 03:32:55
but to me it was obvious that so much of

03:32:52 --> 03:32:57
my life outcomes weren't just a product

03:32:54 --> 03:33:00
of hard work or my family sacrifices

03:32:57 --> 03:33:02
because of course I had the same uh

03:32:59 --> 03:33:05
family as my my siblings

03:33:01 --> 03:33:08
um but the product of State institutions

03:33:04 --> 03:33:11
helping out evening things out uh Public

03:33:07 --> 03:33:13
School District Public Library like all

03:33:10 --> 03:33:15
sorts of after school programs all that

03:33:12 --> 03:33:19
was the domain of the of the state and I

03:33:14 --> 03:33:21
really benefited from it so in a Essence

03:33:18 --> 03:33:24
my core was a Social Democratic belief

03:33:20 --> 03:33:26
the state should we distribute a bit

03:33:23 --> 03:33:28
build in public institutions be an

03:33:25 --> 03:33:30
equalizer now how it became a Marxist

03:33:27 --> 03:33:32
and a socialist was much more random I

03:33:29 --> 03:33:34
was just intellectually interested in it

03:33:31 --> 03:33:37
and eventually I kind of merged the two

03:33:33 --> 03:33:40
together where I merged together my

03:33:36 --> 03:33:42
more pragmatic and practical

03:33:39 --> 03:33:44
interest in day-to-day concerns and

03:33:41 --> 03:33:47
reforms and so on with my loftier

03:33:43 --> 03:33:48
intellectual interest in Marxism into

03:33:46 --> 03:33:51
you know the politics I have today which

03:33:47 --> 03:33:54
I try to kind of balance and do both and

03:33:50 --> 03:33:55
I think a lot of socialists in the

03:33:53 --> 03:33:58
organization that I joined as a teenager

03:33:54 --> 03:34:00
the Democratic socialists of America and

03:33:57 --> 03:34:02
elsewhere uh try to do the two try to

03:33:59 --> 03:34:05
maintain some sort of balanced dream

03:34:01 --> 03:34:05
hair and you know our vision of the

03:34:04 --> 03:34:08
future

03:34:04 --> 03:34:10
what do you think Marx would say if he

03:34:07 --> 03:34:12
were to read your book uh socialist

03:34:09 --> 03:34:16
manifest and do a review

03:34:11 --> 03:34:18
so I think Marx would say that

03:34:15 --> 03:34:20
um my vision of a socialism after

03:34:17 --> 03:34:23
capitalism

03:34:19 --> 03:34:27
aintains key elements of what he would

03:34:22 --> 03:34:30
uh uh the commodity form so a lot of

03:34:26 --> 03:34:31
what Marx was concerned about was

03:34:29 --> 03:34:34
um

03:34:30 --> 03:34:35
What markets do to Human Relationships

03:34:33 --> 03:34:38
um in a negative sense his early

03:34:34 --> 03:34:40
writings especially focus a lot on the

03:34:37 --> 03:34:43
alienation of Labor

03:34:39 --> 03:34:45
um my vision of socialism at least in

03:34:42 --> 03:34:47
the air term a lot about it is about

03:34:44 --> 03:34:49
decommodifying certain sectors so

03:34:46 --> 03:34:51
reducing the market in certain sectors

03:34:48 --> 03:34:53
and reducing alienation

03:34:50 --> 03:34:56
but not eliminating it it is about

03:34:52 --> 03:34:59
eliminating exploitation and oppression

03:34:55 --> 03:35:01
so knowing marks and knowing how

03:34:58 --> 03:35:03
critical he was of certain other

03:35:00 --> 03:35:05
socialist strands and tendencies and he

03:35:02 --> 03:35:06
would often write very snarky notes and

03:35:04 --> 03:35:10
letters to people like Engels being like

03:35:05 --> 03:35:12
this guy Sally he's a total asshole and

03:35:09 --> 03:35:13
then he would send a separate note to

03:35:11 --> 03:35:16
LaSalle saying hey can I borrow five

03:35:12 --> 03:35:18
grand this is actually true he did the

03:35:15 --> 03:35:20
boat he did the both I think the same

03:35:17 --> 03:35:21
like um the same month

03:35:19 --> 03:35:23
um so he would be really good at Twitter

03:35:20 --> 03:35:25
is what you're saying oh he'd be the

03:35:22 --> 03:35:27
best at Twitter and also he used to be a

03:35:24 --> 03:35:29
he was a journalist before with his work

03:35:26 --> 03:35:32
for the New York Tribune

03:35:28 --> 03:35:34
um he was very clever very snarky he

03:35:31 --> 03:35:36
would be awesome at Twitter I think him

03:35:33 --> 03:35:37
and Elon would have a good back and

03:35:35 --> 03:35:39
forth but yeah I think it would be

03:35:36 --> 03:35:42
critical in some parts but I think the

03:35:38 --> 03:35:44
strangest part for him would be

03:35:41 --> 03:35:47
reading the historical sections and

03:35:43 --> 03:35:48
seeing the way in which his ideas which

03:35:46 --> 03:35:50
was fundamentally ideas about human

03:35:47 --> 03:35:51
emancipation

03:35:49 --> 03:35:54
um

03:35:50 --> 03:35:56
were used for for evil for hardship you

03:35:53 --> 03:35:58
know for in in ways that did the

03:35:55 --> 03:36:00
opposite of of emancipated but in some

03:35:57 --> 03:36:01
cases you know enslaved people and I

03:35:59 --> 03:36:02
think he would have

03:36:00 --> 03:36:05
definitely not want to be associated

03:36:01 --> 03:36:07
with them uh he probably would rather be

03:36:04 --> 03:36:11
associated with me than them but even

03:36:06 --> 03:36:11
then uh only begrudgingly

03:36:10 --> 03:36:15
uh

03:36:12 --> 03:36:17
what uh advice would you give to Young

03:36:14 --> 03:36:21
Folks

03:36:16 --> 03:36:23
in high school in college how to have a

03:36:20 --> 03:36:25
career they can be proud of or how to

03:36:22 --> 03:36:27
change the world I think be

03:36:24 --> 03:36:29
intellectually curious

03:36:26 --> 03:36:32
um you know read outside your current

03:36:28 --> 03:36:34
beliefs and understand and read authors

03:36:31 --> 03:36:36
on their own terms

03:36:33 --> 03:36:37
o the worst thing in the world to do is

03:36:35 --> 03:36:40
to read anything especially work a

03:36:36 --> 03:36:41
fiction but anything and try to deduce

03:36:39 --> 03:36:43
the authors

03:36:40 --> 03:36:45
you know backgrounds or politics

03:36:42 --> 03:36:48
whatever else like read it on its own

03:36:44 --> 03:36:50
terms first then you could reread it and

03:36:47 --> 03:36:51
kind of do other examinations or or

03:36:49 --> 03:36:55
whatever else and also read a lot of

03:36:51 --> 03:36:57
history so I started off reading books

03:36:54 --> 03:37:00
like Eric hopis bombs

03:36:56 --> 03:37:03
um uh four books on

03:36:59 --> 03:37:05
um history going from the 1700s all the

03:37:02 --> 03:37:06
way to 1994 the last book is age of

03:37:04 --> 03:37:09
extremes

03:37:05 --> 03:37:11
but I think understanding history gives

03:37:08 --> 03:37:13
you a bird's eye view of everything uh

03:37:10 --> 03:37:16
sociology

03:37:12 --> 03:37:18
um economics everything so these big

03:37:15 --> 03:37:20
sweeping historical books are really

03:37:17 --> 03:37:21
useful to know like everybody should

03:37:19 --> 03:37:24
know basically

03:37:20 --> 03:37:26
ou know what your um or at least like

03:37:23 --> 03:37:28
what decade you know certain was

03:37:25 --> 03:37:29
abolished with decades service slavery

03:37:27 --> 03:37:32
is about abolished you know what century

03:37:28 --> 03:37:33
Magna Carta was you know when the Roman

03:37:31 --> 03:37:36
Empire fell even though it's kind of

03:37:32 --> 03:37:38
debated when the Roman Empire fell all

03:37:35 --> 03:37:40
these I think like being a person with a

03:37:37 --> 03:37:42
general knowledge and general sense of

03:37:39 --> 03:37:44
history and whatever else just makes you

03:37:41 --> 03:37:46
more eclectic and interesting and it's

03:37:43 --> 03:37:48
way better than just like especially a

03:37:45 --> 03:37:50
lot of my Indian friends you know the

03:37:47 --> 03:37:52
obnoxious Indians but the hyper

03:37:49 --> 03:37:55
focus on like uh you got to specialize

03:37:51 --> 03:37:56
and you have to like focus on on math or

03:37:54 --> 03:37:58
engineering whatever you want to do you

03:37:55 --> 03:37:59
just know your field really well but

03:37:57 --> 03:38:01
nothing else like I think there's

03:37:58 --> 03:38:02
omething really too whether you're

03:38:00 --> 03:38:03
getting at school or you're just going

03:38:01 --> 03:38:06
to do it by yourself giving yourself

03:38:03 --> 03:38:08
kind of a liberal arts education

03:38:05 --> 03:38:10
I think there's a lot of power to sort

03:38:07 --> 03:38:12
of having the facts of history in terms

03:38:09 --> 03:38:15
of in time when stuff happen but also

03:38:11 --> 03:38:18
really powerful is knowing spatially

03:38:14 --> 03:38:19
like the geography the where there were

03:38:17 --> 03:38:21
a point in the map and there's

03:38:18 --> 03:38:23
interesting dynamics that happen

03:38:20 --> 03:38:24
throughout history of all the different

03:38:22 --> 03:38:26
nations in Europe of all the different

03:38:23 --> 03:38:31
military conflicts and the expansions

03:38:25 --> 03:38:32
and and the wars and the empires and all

03:38:30 --> 03:38:34
that kind of stuff it really puts into

03:38:31 --> 03:38:37
context how

03:38:33 --> 03:38:38
human history has led to the place

03:38:36 --> 03:38:40
we are today because all the different

03:38:37 --> 03:38:42
geopolitical conflicts we have today

03:38:39 --> 03:38:45
even the politics of the day is grounded

03:38:41 --> 03:38:46
in history maybe less so for the United

03:38:44 --> 03:38:48
States because it has a very young

03:38:45 --> 03:38:50
history but that history even from the

03:38:48 --> 03:38:53
United States is still there right from

03:38:49 --> 03:38:56
the Civil War and understanding that

03:38:52 --> 03:38:57
gives you context to when you tweet

03:38:55 --> 03:39:01
random stuff about

03:38:56 --> 03:39:05
uh this or that person or politician and

03:39:00 --> 03:39:07
so on uh yeah very true

03:39:04 --> 03:39:09
one of the regrets I have

03:39:06 --> 03:39:11
currently is I have perhaps been too

03:39:08 --> 03:39:13
focused on the 20th century

03:39:10 --> 03:39:16
in terms of History

03:39:12 --> 03:39:19
the present in the 20th century

03:39:15 --> 03:39:20
a lot of people write to me that there's

03:39:18 --> 03:39:23
a lot of lessons to be learned in

03:39:19 --> 03:39:24
ancient history as well so not just even

03:39:22 --> 03:39:27
American history but just looking

03:39:23 --> 03:39:30
farther and farther and farther back

03:39:26 --> 03:39:32
yeah that feels like

03:39:29 --> 03:39:35
it's another time it's another place

03:39:31 --> 03:39:36
it's totally has no lessons but then you

03:39:34 --> 03:39:38
remind yourself that it's the same human

03:39:35 --> 03:39:40
beings right yeah and also we're no

03:39:37 --> 03:39:41
smarter than them we just have more

03:39:39 --> 03:39:43
crude knowledge in part because of them

03:39:40 --> 03:39:46
yeah but like you know they're just as

03:39:42 --> 03:39:48
they were just as clever as us you know

03:39:45 --> 03:39:50
uh what do you think is the meaning of

03:39:47 --> 03:39:51
this whole experiment we have going on

03:39:49 --> 03:39:53
Earth

03:39:50 --> 03:39:56
Now what's the meaning of life

03:39:52 --> 03:39:58
well I think there's no broad meaning of

03:39:55 --> 03:40:00
life there's you know it was an accident

03:39:57 --> 03:40:04
but we ourselves need to make our own

03:39:59 --> 03:40:06
meeting and for me a lot of it is about

03:40:03 --> 03:40:08
um posterity trying to do something

03:40:06 --> 03:40:11
worthwhile

03:40:07 --> 03:40:12
on Earth but also leaving

03:40:10 --> 03:40:15
something behind it could just be

03:40:11 --> 03:40:17
relationships with friends or family in

03:40:14 --> 03:40:18
the future maybe having a family and

03:40:16 --> 03:40:20
then kind of like leaving behind that

03:40:17 --> 03:40:22
sort of Legacy

03:40:19 --> 03:40:25
um the little bits of yourself but also

03:40:21 --> 03:40:27
you know but them being able to learn

03:40:24 --> 03:40:29
the same way I have little bits of my my

03:40:26 --> 03:40:32
parents and my my grandparents

03:40:28 --> 03:40:34
um and me and then also I think in a

03:40:31 --> 03:40:36
social sense zooming out from just the

03:40:33 --> 03:40:39
individual and the family

03:40:35 --> 03:40:43
um leaving the World Behind a little

03:40:39 --> 03:40:45
better you know uh I would love to be a

03:40:42 --> 03:40:47
part of a movement that created a world

03:40:44 --> 03:40:49
with a little bit less suffering a

03:40:46 --> 03:40:51
little bit less oppression or

03:40:48 --> 03:40:54
exploitation or or whatever else

03:40:51 --> 03:40:55
um that's really why I'm uh why I'm a

03:40:53 --> 03:40:58
socialist you know it's not about

03:40:54 --> 03:41:00
stopping your fingers and curing the

03:40:57 --> 03:41:02
world of everything in one in one go but

03:41:00 --> 03:41:04
it is about I think

03:41:01 --> 03:41:06
putting our lives giving our lives some

03:41:03 --> 03:41:07
sort of meaning and purpose and you

03:41:05 --> 03:41:10
don't have to be a socialist to do that

03:41:06 --> 03:41:13
you could just do it at the you know at

03:41:09 --> 03:41:15
he micro level in your own day-to-day

03:41:12 --> 03:41:18
interactions but I just feel like life

03:41:14 --> 03:41:20
has no good meaning without

03:41:17 --> 03:41:21
um without thinking of posterity in the

03:41:19 --> 03:41:24
future

03:41:20 --> 03:41:27
and I have to say thank you for doing so

03:41:23 --> 03:41:28
thank you for caring about the struggle

03:41:26 --> 03:41:32
of the people in the world through ideas

03:41:27 --> 03:41:34
that are bold and I think challenging

03:41:31 --> 03:41:36
for a lot of people in a time when

03:41:33 --> 03:41:38
socialism

03:41:35 --> 03:41:40
is something that can be attacked

03:41:37 --> 03:41:42
aggressively by large numbers of people

03:41:39 --> 03:41:45
still persevering and still exploring

03:41:41 --> 03:41:47
those ideas and seeing what of those

03:41:44 --> 03:41:49
ideas can make for a better world that's

03:41:46 --> 03:41:50
beautiful to see Bhaskar thank you so

03:41:48 --> 03:41:52
much for talking today thank you for all

03:41:49 --> 03:41:54
the work you do I can't wait to see what

03:41:51 --> 03:41:56
you do next I appreciate it and uh yeah

ese conversations and to your audience

space where you know people can debate

and think at length and don't have to

brother
